                     Misa Jennifer Fox
                     27 Kedron Road
                     WOOLOOWIN QLD 4030

                     Dear Miss Fox,


                               The following info1a1ation is aupplied in response to your
                     enquiry regarding unusual aerial sightings.

                               The investigation of reports of unusual aerial aighting in
                     Australia is carried out by the Royal Australian Air Force.    Preliminary
                     investigation of the reports is conducted at th near et Royal Australian
                     Air Force base.   The reports are then forwarded to the D partment of
                     Defence (Air Force Off ice) where an inv stigating team co plet a the
                     investigation.

                                Between January 1960 and Decenber 1974 882 a1ght1ngs were
                     reported to the Royal Australian Air Force.      90 per cent of the sightings
                     were attributed to causes which included aircraft, eat llit e, met ors, apace
                     debris re-entry, meteorological balloons, stars and planets.      Seven per c nt
                     of reports were either received too lat or did not provide sufficient
                     information to pel'lllit proper analysia and evaluation.   Three per cent of
                     reports wer attributed to unknown causes.

                               The Unit d Kingdom Air Ministry h s stated that on an average.
                     90 per cent of the sightings it inveatigatee are explainable by caus a ranging
                     from meteorological balloons and meteor• to eircraft lights.   The other
                     10 per cent probably r£1aain unexplained bee ua of lack of reliable information.
                 •

                               The University of Colorado, under contract to th USAF, spent             t
                     two years processing and investigating all American reports.    The findings
                     were published in 1968, by Bant   Books, as the 'Condon Report'.    The general
                     conclusion was that 'nothing has co   fr   the study of UFOs i the past
                     21 years that has adeed to scientific knowledge' and that 'furth r    tenaive
                     study of UFOs probably cannot be ju tified in the expectation that scienc
                     will b advanced thereb•'·    A pahel of 6mllnent scientiata, chosen by the US
                     Nation 1 AcadeNy of Sciences, has ex ined. and endorsed the Condon R port.


                                                                                        /The USAF

                                   The USAF 'Project Blue Book' investigation of unidentified
                         aerial objects between 1953 and 1965 analysed 7,641 UFO reports.   The
                         conclusions were:
 ..
                                      a.   80 per cent of sightings were natural phenomena,
                                           hoaxes, birds or man-made objects;

                                      b.   17 per cent of sightings provided insufficient data
                                           to permit thorough ~alysis and evaluation; and

                                      c.   three per cent were unidentified.

                                   United States and Soviet space exploration bas found no evidence
                         to support theotheory of life on planets in our solar system.   The Mariner
                         series of space exploration to Mara appears to have proved it a 'dead' planet.
                         The only other source of extra-terrestrial life, therefore, would have to be
                         in another solar system.   The nearest visible star to Earth is Alpha Centaur!
                         which is about 25 million million miles away.   Even if lif forms existed
                         there and were capable of space travel at speeds currently known to man, ie
                         about 25,000 mph they would take about 115,000 years to reach Earth.    Consequently,
                         the probability of extra-terrestrial life forms visiting Earth is extreri1ely
                         remote.

                                   Enclosed are five s11111111aries of unusual aerial sightings for the
                         period January 1960 to December 1974.

                                   REPORT OF AN UNUSUAL AERIAL SIGHTING


               Reference:    A.     HQ OC ASI 3/A/5


               1.        Enclosed please find a report of an unusual aerial s i ghting near
               Jondaryan on 15th March 1976.

               2.         The sighting was macte by l"lrs B. f{ . r,:arsh of ''IVinarda'' who has completed
               a r eport in accordance with Annex A to Reference A and who has also provided a
               supplementary statement and a sketch of the object sighted.

               3.           When this sighting was first reported to me I noted the 15th of March
               as being a Monday .     The School of Army Aviation conducts ni ght fl ying operati ons
               each 1-'londay evening.   I have established with ATC staff at Oakey that all flying
               had ceased before the time of the sighting on t he night of 15th March 1976.

               4.        Mrs }1arsh is known to me personally and I have no hesi t ation in forward-
               ing the enclosed report for your further action as necessary.

"                            Initial investigation of your :report of an 11101m2aJ aerial
                  sighting on 6 November 1976 indicates that the ost probable explanation
                  is the planet Venus.       Reports of this nature a1 e not nnco11011on,
                  par•ticularly if the planet is low on the horizon.       Often in fact
                  unusual atmospheric cor•di tions or atmospheric pollution (dust etc) can
                  oa11se these la•oger planets to appear m•ilti oolo,11ed or cha'Jl8e colo11r
                  even.    It is not 1moonn11on for the to appear to move either (apar•t
                  frorn their nat\1 ••al progxession across the sky that is).     This illusion
                  can be attrib~ted to either the observer watching the object too
                  intently or relative motion of the backg1'01md or observer.         High level
                  cloud moving in f%'0lll the west that evening could have obso111 ed the
                  planet. occasionally.       The reported direction of yo,ir sighting
                  approximates that of Venus on that evening end at that time.

                                      REPORT OF UNUSUAL AERIAL SIGHTINGS


             1•        Enclosed are copies of reports of tv10 separate instances of
             unusual aerial sightings.

             2.        Both reports were made by experienced Air Traffic Control personnel
             and concern sightings made from the control tower at Army Aviation Centre.
             Part 3 of the reports is designed to cover both sightings.

             3.        It is requested that this headquarters be informed of the results
             of your investigations.

                Dear Sir,
                             Further to 011r telephone conversation or 24 September 1976,
                enclosed please find an 'Observers Report' lll1i oh you y wish to complete
                in zespect to the sighting made by yo111•self and yo11r companion on the
                night of 23 September 1976.                                          ·    •
                                                                                              -             ••



                             From yo111• verbal account of the incident to me and to
                Wing Cona•1Snder 13ibby, I am confident that what you aaw was in tact the                        '
                fo11r F111C aircraft which overflew Brisbane on that night.       1he time of
                sighting end that of the flight coincide, as does the observed directions
                of sighting and movement of the objects.       The red lights first seen
                would have been the airczaft navigation and anti-collision lights, and
                the subsequent amber light was fuel igniting behind the aircraft as they
                climbed away.      The flight profile was a climb from about 5000' to
                15000'.    'l'his, together with the distance between yo111•selves and th
                airczaft position and the direction of flight, is why there vas no
                apparent noise.      The clear night also would have JM de the object seem
                much closer than it was.       The irxegular pattern of the lights you
                cODDented on would ba:ve been ca.11sed by the pexspective from yo11r position.

                           I hope these brief c~•D•••ents provide you with a satisf'actory
                answer on this matter, however, should 70u o desire, please feel free to
                complete the enclosed pro-fonaa and return it to the above add• ees.

                       been received and investigat on indicates the ost probable
                       cause to be a star.
                                During the ti e of th sighting the star Spica,
                      which was the briglltest star in the area, closely follo ed the
                      movement described in your report.    The unusual brightness
                      was probabl y caused by atmospheric pollution or unusual
                      atmospheric condit ions, both of hich are kno n to produce
                      the effects you described.

               •

              ·D ear Mrs Mccrea,
                               Investigation of your report of an
              unusual aerial sighting on 23 March 1976 indicates
              that the ·most logical explanation is the planet
              Venus.    The second sighting of a large star on
              2 April 1976 is also considered to be Venus and
              the nort~erly movement of the planet between the
              two sightings is contirmed by star charts.
                        Although the planet should have been
              visible with the same degree of magnitude on both
              occasions 11nusual atmospheric conditions or
              atmospheric pollution trapped below a te perat11re
              inversion may have been present during the first
              sighting.   These conditions are known to cause
              greatly increased magnitude and unushal colours in
              stars and planets.
                          Trusting that this explanation is satisfactory.
                                                  Yours faithfully,


                                                        '
                                                  (M. GRETTON)
                                                  Flight Lieutenant
                                                  for Officer Commanding

                  •                                       Description/Sketch of Object

                              •

                                             When first sighting the object, I could not seem to focus my
                                      eyes properly on it. If you fold the drawing over, this is how it
                                      fir s t seemed to me. The colour was like a brilliant light or a mass
                                      of stars together . It would have been about 1 the size of a full moon
                                      which was overhead. It was not the moon as it was above me at 90~ this
                                      morming it was in the last quarter.                         ·
                                           I watched it for about 5 min., then went inside the house for
                                      4 to 5 min. to cover the children. I returned to see if it was still
                                      there. It was and there appeared to be a haze over the whole image.
                  •
                                      The sky was still clear in that particular area. If the object moved
                                      it did so very slowly.
                                           I am no artist but this is ·as close to the object I can get •

                                      UNUSUAL AE:RtAL SIGHTINGS


               1.        Enclosed a:> e a newspaper cutting, letter and report i:elating
               to an unusual aerial sighting by Mrs I.M. Jones at Burleigh Heads on
               25 Jam1ary 1976.     The sighting is well documented and the acc111acy
               of reporting has been verified in a telephone interview with Mrs Jones.
               The impression gained over the telephone was that Mrs Jones is a
               rational person and both her and her h11sband were concerned at the
               sighting.
               2.          The weather at the time was fine, no cloud and a light northerly
               wind.      A trough was situated west ~r the ranges.

               3.       The following possible causes for the sighting have been
               investigated and disco11nted for the rea.sons stateds
                           a.   ilrcra.ft. There were no civil or military
                                aircraft operating in thtt a.Na t the ti e.
                          b.    Stars and Planets.   There were no sisnificant
                                stars or planets to the south.
                           c.   Satellites and Space Debris.    These have been
                                disco>>nted due to the length of time or the
                                sighting.
                           d.   Meteorological Ealloons.   A balloon waa
                                released fro Eagle Fa•1D at 0200 h1'8 but the
                                identification light xemains on only for a
                                short period until radar lock-on is achieved.
                                Additionally 50 minutes has elapsed from
                                balloon release time to ti e of the sighting.
               4.       The attached map, showing place and direction of the sighting,
               does not indicate any 11nusual terxai n features which may have bearing
               on the sighting.
               5.        The names and addresses of the other two witnesses to a 'similar'
               occun:ence at about the same time a1e not known and therefore further
               reports to substantiate Mrs Jones' report cannot be obtained.    If
               required the police at Burleigh Heads can be contacted to elicit further
               info11netion or obtain names and addresses, if available, of other witnesses.

                HQ/5/113/Air(15)                          12 February 1976

    Mr M. Price
    9 Kennion St,
    OXFORD PARK QLD 4053

    Dear Sir,
             With reference to the report of an unusual a rial sighting
    on 8 February 1976 made by yourself and your daughter, a full .
    investigation of the matter will be conducted if you wish to complete
    the attached form and r4turn it to the RAAF Base at Amberley.
             A number of possible causes of the sightings have been
    considered since receipt of the report.   Of these causes aircraft,
    satellites and space debris re-entry have been discounted.   Neither
    military nor civil aircraft were operating in the area at the time
    and whilst sightings of a similar nature can sometimes be attributed
    to satellites or the re-entry of space debris, these sightings are
    usually of a shorter duration than those reported.
             A probable cause would s eem bo be one of several minor stars
    visible in the east at the ti••e, with pollutants in the l air or unusual
    atmospheric conditions producing the effects described.
                                              Yours faithfully,


                                                          '
                                                      (M. GRETTON)
                                                       Flight Lieutenant
                                                       for Officer Commanding

                                      UNIDENTIFIED FLYING OBJECT
                                      REPORTE31 13Y itR K. W.KRUGER


             1.       Report. At approximately 1330 hours o 19 September 1975,
             Mr Mathew William Kruger, aged 66 y-ea1·s, dai   tanner, of' Colleyville
             via Harri sville, Queensland, (telephone: &1. 6652) called to the gual'd
             room to xeport the sighting ot an unidentified flying object (UEu).

             2.        Date of Sightipg. Mr Kruger could not gi. ve the exact date
             of' the UFO sighting. However, he said that it occurred on the same
             night as the sighting of a UFO by a woman in I.a:! dley sometime in
             May 1975. This sighting was apparently publicised in the media.

             3.       This report was not made earlier because Kruger• s wif'e did
             not wish him to do so; but he was in Ipswich without his wif'e on
             19 September so he took the opportunity to call in to Amberley.
             4. ·     First Sighting• The UFO was first sighted by Kruger from a
             position north-east of his house - which faces almost east-west - as
             he was driving from his bottom paddock towal'ds his house.

             5.       Time of First Sighting. ~e time of the first sighting was
             placed at around 17j) hours. It was aro11nd dusk and Kruger was back
             in his house appro:rlmately five minutes before the start of the
             6 o'clock news.
             6.       Weather. The weather at the time was fine. The sky was
             clear and therewere no clouds.

             7.        Object Description,. The object was described as being a
             ' man made craft', about ID' long and 5' to 6• in diameter. It had
             two rot1nd white lights neatly placed in the rear, and had a fin where
             •the wing should have been. • There was no reflection fl'OID the two
               lights and although the object looked verr dalit no dis~tinct colour
             could be discerned.                          .

             8.       Method of Proptil~on,. Mr Kruger could not specify the method
             of propulsion of the object. He said it emitted a rumbling,olatterr
             noise. He did not think it sounded like a jet engine and yet it had
             no propellers. On the other hand, there was a long d&Iit flame which
             came from the :li:>nt of the object and travelled aro11nd the body towal'ds
             the rear from the front.                                            .

             9.       Direction of Object.        When first seen, the object was estimated
             to be travelling east.

                                                                          /10.    Hei  t
National Archives of Australia                                        NAA: BP990 1, '"'' 113/AIR PART 1
                                                -2-


              10.      Height of Object. llr Kruger estimated the height ot the object
              to be within 100'. As he co11u••ented, he could have 'hit it with a stick.•.

              11.      Speed of Object. The speed of the object was likened to that
              of a light aircraft on take off. llr Kruger has apparently spent some
              time watching light aircraft _a t Toowoomba and based his judgment of
              the object• s speed on his observation ot these aircraft.

              12.       .Angle of Object. The object was seen through the wimscreen
              ot Kruger' s vehicle and he esti~ed it's angle to the horizon to be
              between 6':> and 70 degrees. The angle of the object was decreasing all
              the time.

              13.     Period ot· Observation.       The object was in view for appro:timately
              six secoms.

              14.      Last Sighting. The object was last sighted dropping towards
              a bill on Kruger• s property. The dark flame cut out and the object
              disappeared.

              15.      Witnesses.   There were no other witnesses to the sighting•.

              16.       Person B;eporting. Mr Kruger appeared to be a very serious
              and - al though he was not asked - a sober type of person. He does not
              seem to enjoy good health and, from one comment, could be a religious
              man. He was certainly convinced that he saw 'something which bad
              no right to be there.•.

              17.      Sketch pt Object.   A sketch of the object, drawn by Mr Kruger,
              is attached as Annex A.
              18.       Could this report please be forwarded to Squadron Leader ~
              BertrAJD, Headquarters RAAF Amberley.

                         REPORT OF UNIDENTIFIED FLYING Qi3JECT
                        On the 27th August, 1975, Mrs D. Kennedy of 13 Creek Road,
              Currumbin, telephoned the following report to our administrative officer
              at Coolangatta .
                        ''Mrs Kennedy reported that she had sighted an unusual object
              in the sky outside of her residence at approximately 9.30 p.m. on
              26th August 1975.
                         She had just gone to bed at approxima.tely 9.30 p.m., closed
              her eyes and commenced saying her prayers when she heard a loud noise
              outside her house. She opened her eyes and looked through the c11rtains
              of her bedroom window in a southerly direction and saw a red ball shaped
              object approximately 20 feet in diameter with a red glow radiating all
              around it, and smoke coming out of the top. She pulled back the
              c11rtain and watched the object which hovered just above the house across
              the street , for a period of about three minutes.

                         The object then descended a short distance, the glow disappeared
               leaving the object in darkness with the exception of vertical slots of
               light which appeared to ema.nate from the inside of the object. The object
               was still visible as a dark ball with the mentioned slots of light.

                         The object then moved slowly in an easterly direction on a
               horizontal plane towards Currumbin Hill and then turned south towards
               Tugun. She lost sight of it behind a house arnno further observation                                               •


               was made''•

                         Mrs Kennedy was unable to describe the noise made by the
               object. A n11mber of different types of sounds were suggested to her but her
               stock answer to all suggestions was ''it sounded like something out of
               this world''•
                         A check of this Departmentts records has failed to provide any
               evidence of notified civil movements in the Currtimbin area at the time of
               sighting.
                         The report is forwarded for any action you ma.y wish to take.
